<a id="session-04-01"></a>
### Session 4.1: Putting a Model to Work: Save It, Score Every Night, Watch for Drift, and Fall Back Safely

![Session 4.1 Map](assets/session_04_01_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_04_01_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_04_01_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 13 (115/12/06 - 115/12/12) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)
* **How this session is organized:** first the problem, then the result, then how a saved model, a nightly job, and a drift check produced that result, step by step with code. The three exercises come last.

* **1. The Problem:**
  * In Session 3.4, the music app built a model that gives each user a chance of cancelling, and chose to send a "come back" coupon at a chance of `0.3` or more.
  * A model in a student's script helps nobody. It creates value only when it runs **every night by itself**: new users come in, the model scores them, and the coupons go out before morning.
  * Tonight is in the middle of final exams. Many students still open the app, but they listen much less than usual.
  * The head of the app's data team asks: how do we run the coupon model every night without retraining it each time, how will we know when tonight's users no longer look like the users it learned from, and what happens if the model file is missing?

* **2. The Result:**
  * The model was trained **once** and saved to a file. Tonight's job loads the file, scores the 10 new users, and decides the coupons in a fraction of a second:

    | Tonight | Result |
    |---|---|
    | Users scored | `10` |
    | Coupons to send (chance `0.3` or more) | `5`: `USR-401`, `USR-403`, `USR-405`, `USR-406`, `USR-407` |
    | Expected cancellations without coupons (sum of the chances) | about `4.0` |

  * A drift check compares tonight's users with the training users:

    | Feature | Training average | Tonight's average | Change | Drift? |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | `Weeks_Inactive_Count` | `3.1` | `2.9` | `-5.7`% | no |
    | `Monthly_Minutes_Count` | `1,361.8` | `780.0` | `-42.7`% | **yes** |

  * **Answer 1:** save the trained model once with `joblib`; every night, load it and score. No retraining, no person needed.
  * **Answer 2:** tonight's users listen 43% less than the users the model learned from. The coupons still go out, but the night is flagged for a person to review: during exam season, low minutes may not mean that a user is about to leave.
  * **Answer 3:** if the model file is missing, the job switches to a simple, safe rule (a coupon for 3 or more weeks inactive, Session 3.1) instead of crashing (Exercise 2).
  * **How it was done:** three short steps with `joblib` and pandas. Sections 6 to 8 do them one by one.

* **3. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "How does a model get from training to daily use?": describe a decision system (data in, checks, model, action), and save and reload a trained model with `joblib`
  2. "Is tonight's data still like the training data?": detect data drift by comparing feature averages
  3. "What if something breaks at night?": read a run log for warning signs, and keep the job running with a fallback rule when the model file is missing

* **4. Key Ideas: System, Drift, Fallback:**
  * **Train once, use many times.** Training is done rarely, because it needs the past users and their answers. Scoring is done every night, on new users. So the trained model is **saved** to a file once, and the nightly job **loads** it.
  * **A decision system** turns predictions into actions automatically. Every night it runs four stages as one **batch** (a group of records processed together):

    | Stage | What happens | In the music app |
    |---|---|---|
    | Ingestion | collect the day's data | the users to check tonight |
    | Validation | check the data before use | all columns present, no empty cells |
    | Scoring | the model gives each record a chance | the chance of cancelling, from 0 to 1 |
    | Action | a business rule turns chances into actions | a coupon at a chance of `0.3` or more |

    ![The nightly decision system: train once and save the model; every night, ingest tonight's users, validate them, score them with the loaded model, and send coupons; a run log records every stage](assets/session_04_01_fig_decision_system.png)

  * **Batch and real-time.** Batch scoring runs on a schedule, such as every night, for many users at once: cheap, and fine when the decision can wait until morning. Real-time scoring answers one request within milliseconds, for example while a user waits for a recommendation: faster, but more expensive to build and run.
  * **Data drift.** A model is only reliable on data like the data it learned from. When the new data changes, for example during exam season or a holiday, the model's chances can become wrong. A simple check: compare each feature's average tonight with its training average, and flag a change of more than 20% in either direction.
  * **Monitoring.** A **run log** records every night's stages: how many records each one handled, how long it took (its **latency**, in milliseconds; 1,000 ms = 1 second), and the accuracy on users whose answer is now known. Rising latency or falling accuracy is an early warning (Exercise 1).
  * **Fallback.** A simple, safe rule used when the model cannot run, so that the business keeps going while someone fixes the problem.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * Three files (all fictional teaching data):
    * Training data: [data/session_03_04_dataset.csv](data/session_03_04_dataset.csv), the 40 users of Session 3.4 with their known answers (`Cancelled_Binary`).
    * Tonight's users: [data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv](data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv), 10 new users during final exams, with `User_ID`, `Weeks_Inactive_Count`, and `Monthly_Minutes_Count`, and no answer column.
    * Run log: [data/session_04_01_dataset.csv](data/session_04_01_dataset.csv), 20 rows: 5 nights x 4 stages, for Exercise 1.
  * Run log columns:
    * `Run_Date`: The night of the run, `2026-12-07` to `2026-12-11`.
    * `Pipeline_Stage`: `Ingestion`, `Validation`, `Scoring`, or `Action`.
    * `Records_Count`: Records handled by the stage. For `Action`, the number of coupons sent.
    * `Latency_Ms`: Time the stage took, in milliseconds.
    * `Model_Accuracy`: Accuracy on users whose answer is now known. Filled only for the `Scoring` stage; empty for the others.
  * Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE: activate your `.venv` (Python Setup Clinic), name each script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S41_step_1.py`), run it with `python` followed by the file name, and check for return code `0`. `joblib` comes with Scikit-Learn, so there is nothing new to install. Step 1 creates a `models/` folder next to your scripts.

* **6. Step 1: Train Once and Save:**
  * *Goal:* train the coupon model on all 40 users once, and save it to a file that the nightly job can load.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S41_step_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: all 40 users with known answers (Session 3.4)
    import os
    import joblib
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    train = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_04_dataset.csv", index_col="User_ID")
    features = ["Weeks_Inactive_Count", "Monthly_Minutes_Count"]

    # 2. Process: train once on every user, then save the trained model to a file
    model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
    model.fit(train[features], train["Cancelled_Binary"])
    os.makedirs("models", exist_ok=True)
    joblib.dump(model, "models/coupon_model.joblib")

    # 3. Output: where the model went, and how it does on its own training users
    accuracy = model.score(train[features], train["Cancelled_Binary"])
    print("Saved: models/coupon_model.joblib")
    print(f"Training accuracy: {accuracy:.2f}")
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * The model is the same pipeline as Session 3.4: scale, then classify. In Session 3.4, half of the users were kept aside to **test** the model. Now that it has passed its test, it is trained on **all** 40 users, so that it learns from every example.
    * `os.makedirs("models", exist_ok=True)` creates a `models/` folder; `exist_ok=True` means "no error if it is already there".
    * `joblib.dump(model, path)` writes the whole trained pipeline, the scaler and the classifier together, into one file.
    * `model.score(...)` gives the accuracy on the training users (Session 3.3).
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
    Saved: models/coupon_model.joblib
    Training accuracy: 0.90
    ```

    * The model file now sits in `models/`. You can see it in the File Explorer, Panel `[1]`.
    * `0.90` is measured on the users the model learned from, so it is optimistic (Session 3.3); the honest test was done in Session 3.4.
  * *Common mistake:* saving only the classifier, without the scaler. The nightly job would then score unscaled minutes in the thousands with a classifier that learned from scaled values, and every chance would be wrong. Saving the whole pipeline keeps the two together.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`os.path.exists()`):*
    * New tool: `os.path.exists(path)` is `True` if a file exists. Why: the nightly job should check for the model file before it loads it (Exercise 2).
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(os.path.______("models/coupon_model.joblib"))`
    * Check: `True`.

* **7. Step 2: The Nightly Job:**
  * *Goal:* load the saved model, give each of tonight's 10 users a chance of cancelling, decide the coupons at `0.3`, and save the decisions.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S41_step_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the saved model and tonight's new users
    import joblib
    import pandas as pd
    model = joblib.load("models/coupon_model.joblib")
    tonight = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv", index_col="User_ID")
    features = ["Weeks_Inactive_Count", "Monthly_Minutes_Count"]

    # 2. Process: score every user, then apply the threshold chosen in Session 3.4
    chance = model.predict_proba(tonight[features])[:, 1]
    tonight["Chance"] = chance.round(2)
    tonight["Coupon"] = (chance >= 0.3).astype(int)
    tonight.to_csv("data/coupons_tonight.csv")

    # 3. Output: tonight's decisions
    print(tonight[["Chance", "Coupon"]])
    print("Coupons to send:", tonight["Coupon"].sum(), "of", len(tonight))
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * `joblib.load(path)` reads the trained pipeline back from the file, ready to predict: no data, no training.
    * `predict_proba(...)[:, 1]` gives each user's chance of cancelling (Session 3.4). The scaler inside the pipeline uses the **training** averages, as Session 3.2 requires.
    * The coupon decision uses the exact chances; `.round(2)` is only for the printed column.
    * `tonight.to_csv("data/coupons_tonight.csv")` saves the decisions, with the two features, for the next system, the one that sends the coupons: this is the **action** stage.
    * `tonight[["Chance", "Coupon"]]` prints only the two new columns; the features are in the data file.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
             Chance  Coupon
    User_ID                
    USR-401    0.32       1
    USR-402    0.05       0
    USR-403    0.72       1
    USR-404    0.19       0
    USR-405    0.48       1
    USR-406    0.78       1
    USR-407    0.94       1
    USR-408    0.23       0
    USR-409    0.07       0
    USR-410    0.20       0
    Coupons to send: 5 of 10
    ```

    * 5 coupons tonight. `USR-407` (6 weeks inactive, 150 minutes) is the clearest case, at `0.94`.
    * `USR-401` gets a coupon at `0.32` although it was inactive only 2 weeks: it played just 250 minutes.
    * `USR-410` gets none, although it was inactive 3 weeks: it still played 1,650 minutes. Exercise 2 shows that the simple rule would decide the other way for both users.
  * *Picture it:* tonight's 10 users and the coupon threshold.

    ![Tonight's 10 users with their chance of cancelling and the coupon threshold at 0.3: five users get a coupon](assets/session_04_01_fig_tonight.png)

  * *Common mistake:* retraining the model inside the nightly job. Tonight's users have no answers yet, so there is nothing to learn from; the job only **loads and scores**.
  * *Quick Check 2 (expected cancellations):*
    * New tool: the sum of the chances is the number of cancellations to expect if no coupons are sent. Why: a single number for tonight's risk.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Expected cancellations:", round(chance.___(), 1))`
    * Check: `Expected cancellations: 4.0`.

* **8. Step 3: Check for Drift:**
  * *Goal:* compare the average of each feature tonight with its average in the training data, and flag a change of more than 20%.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S41_step_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the training users and tonight's users
    import pandas as pd
    train = pd.read_csv("data/session_03_04_dataset.csv", index_col="User_ID")
    tonight = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv", index_col="User_ID")
    features = ["Weeks_Inactive_Count", "Monthly_Minutes_Count"]

    # 2. Process: compare each feature's average tonight with its training average
    drift = pd.DataFrame({"Train_Avg": train[features].mean(),
                          "Tonight_Avg": tonight[features].mean()})
    change = drift["Tonight_Avg"] - drift["Train_Avg"]
    drift["Change_Pct"] = change / drift["Train_Avg"] * 100
    drift["Drift_Flag"] = drift["Change_Pct"].abs() > 20

    # 3. Output: the averages, the change in percent, and the flag
    print(drift.round(1))
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * `train[features].mean()` gives one average per feature; so does `tonight[features].mean()`. Putting both in one DataFrame lines them up by feature name.
    * The change in percent is (tonight - training) / training x 100.
    * `.abs()` turns a negative change into a positive one, so that a large drop and a large rise are both flagged.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
                           Train_Avg  Tonight_Avg  Change_Pct  Drift_Flag
    Weeks_Inactive_Count         3.1          2.9        -5.7       False
    Monthly_Minutes_Count     1361.8        780.0       -42.7        True
    ```

    * Weeks inactive barely changed (`-5.7`%): students still open the app.
    * Minutes fell by `42.7`%: exam season. The model learned that low minutes mean "about to leave", which may not be true this week.
    * The coupons can still go out, but the night is flagged for a person to review, and the team should watch whether these users really cancel.
  * *Picture it:* the minutes of the 40 training users and of tonight's 10 users, with their averages.

    ![Monthly minutes of the 40 training users and of tonight's 10 users: tonight's average is 42.7% lower](assets/session_04_01_fig_drift.png)

  * *Common mistake:* trusting the model's chances without looking at the data. A model never says "I have not seen data like this"; the drift check has to say it.
  * *Quick Check 3 (a stricter rule):*
    * Tool: the `20` in `drift["Change_Pct"].abs() > 20`.
    * Task: change the rule to `> _` to flag any change of more than 5%.
    * Check: both features are now `True`. A stricter rule raises more alarms; each team chooses the level that it can act on.

* **9. Summary: The Nightly System:**
  * The three steps fit together into one job that runs every night:

    ![The nightly job: load the saved model, validate tonight's data, check drift, score and decide, save the decisions; if the model file is missing, use the fallback rule; flag the night for review if anything drifted or the fallback was used](assets/session_04_01_fig_nightly_job.png)

    | Step | What we did | Why it matters |
    |---|---|---|
    | 1. Train once and save | `joblib.dump()` of the whole pipeline | the nightly job needs no training data |
    | 2. Load, score, decide | 5 coupons for 10 users at `0.3` | predictions become actions every night |
    | 3. Check drift | minutes `-42.7`%: flagged | the model warns nobody by itself |
    | Exercises | run log alerts, a fallback rule, one nightly function | the job keeps working and reports its own doubts |

  * *Remember:* train once, save, load every night; check that tonight looks like training; keep a fallback; log every run.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Scikit-Learn: Model Persistence (joblib)](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/model_persistence.html).
  * [joblib: dump and load](https://joblib.readthedocs.io/en/stable/persistence.html).
  * [Python: os.path.exists](https://docs.python.org/3/library/os.path.html#os.path.exists).
  * [Google Cloud: MLOps: Continuous Delivery and Automation Pipelines in Machine Learning](https://cloud.google.com/architecture/mlops-continuous-delivery-and-automation-pipelines-in-machine-learning).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Read the Run Log:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S41_monitoring.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S41_monitoring.py`).
  * *Step 2: Find the Nights That Need Attention:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the run log, 5 nights x 4 stages
    import pandas as pd
    log = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_01_dataset.csv")

    # 2. Process: health rules for the Scoring stage, then totals per night
    scoring = log[log["Pipeline_Stage"] == "Scoring"].copy()
    scoring["Alert"] = (scoring["Latency_Ms"] > 900) | (scoring["Model_Accuracy"] < 0.88)
    nightly = log.groupby("Run_Date").agg(
        Total_Latency_Ms=("Latency_Ms", "sum"),
        Users_In=("Records_Count", "first"),
    )

    # 3. Output: the Scoring stage per night, then the nightly totals
    print(scoring[["Run_Date", "Latency_Ms", "Model_Accuracy", "Alert"]])
    print(nightly)
    ```
    * `.copy()` makes `scoring` an independent table, so adding the `Alert` column does not change `log`.
    * `|` means "or": a night is flagged if either rule is broken. Each rule sits in its own brackets (Session 1.4).
    * `"first"` in `.agg()` takes the first value of each night: the `Ingestion` row's count.
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, describe the trend in latency and accuracy across the 5 nights, and what the team should do next.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S41_monitoring.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* alerts on `2026-12-10` (latency `910`, accuracy `0.871`) and `2026-12-11` (`980`, `0.846`). The total latency is `1470` ms on the first two nights, then rises every night to `1730` ms, while the users coming in grow from `52000` to `61500`. More users and falling accuracy together point to drift: the model should be checked, and possibly retrained on recent users.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Survive a Missing Model File:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S41_fallback.py`, make the nightly job of Step 2 keep working when the model file cannot be found.
  * *Task 1 (Read the FileNotFoundError & AI Diagnosis):* In a copy of Step 2, change the path to `"models/coupon_modle.joblib"` (a typo). The script stops with `FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or directory: 'models/coupon_modle.joblib'`. Capture the traceback (Windows: `Win + Shift + S`; Mac: `Cmd + Ctrl + Shift + 4`), paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat), and ask how to check for a file before loading it.
  * *Task 2 (Add a Fallback Rule):* Store the path in a variable `model_path`. If `os.path.exists(model_path)`, load the model and decide as in Step 2, and set `method = "model"`. Otherwise, use the fallback rule of Session 3.1: a coupon when `Weeks_Inactive_Count` is 3 or more, and set `method = "fallback rule"`. Print the method used.
  * *Task 3 (Compare):* Run the script once with the typo and once with the correct path, and print the IDs of the users who get a coupon each time: `list(tonight.index[tonight["Coupon"] == 1])`.
  * *Success Check:*
    * With the typo, the fallback rule sends 5 coupons: `USR-403`, `USR-405`, `USR-406`, `USR-407`, `USR-410`.
    * With the correct path, the model sends 5 coupons: `USR-401`, `USR-403`, `USR-405`, `USR-406`, `USR-407`.
    * Same number, different users: the rule misses `USR-401` (only 250 minutes) and wastes a coupon on `USR-410` (1,650 minutes). The script ends with return code `0` both times: the fallback keeps the app running, but it is weaker than the model, so the missing file must still be fixed quickly.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - One Nightly Function:**
  * *Goal:* Combine everything into one function that the data team could schedule to run every night: validate, check drift, score (or fall back), act, and report.
  * *What you need:*
    * Files: `data/session_03_04_dataset.csv` (training), `data/session_04_01_new_batch.csv` (tonight), and `models/coupon_model.joblib` (from Step 1).
    * Script name: `YourName_S41_nightly.py`.
    * Tools you already know: functions (Session 2.4), `assert` (Session 2.3), `os.path.exists()`, `joblib.load()`, `.predict_proba()`, the drift table of Step 3, and the fallback rule of Exercise 2.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Write `run_nightly(batch_path, model_path, train_path)` that returns a dictionary summary.
    2. **Validation:** check with `assert` that the batch has both feature columns and no empty cells in them.
    3. **Drift:** compute the change in percent of each feature's average, as in Step 3, and collect the names of the features whose absolute change is above 20.
    4. **Scoring:** if the model file exists, send a coupon at a chance of `0.3` or more; otherwise, use the fallback rule of Exercise 2.
    5. **Action:** save the batch with `Coupon` and `Method` columns to `data/nightly_decisions.csv`.
    6. **Report:** return `{"records": ..., "coupons": ..., "method": ..., "drift_features": [...], "needs_review": ...}`, where `needs_review` is `True` if any feature drifted **or** the fallback was used.
    7. Call the function twice, once with the correct model path and once with a wrong path, and print both summaries.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Checking that columns exist: `set(features).issubset(batch.columns)` is `True` when every name in `features` is a column of `batch`.
    * A list of names from a True/False column: `list(drift.index[drift["Drift_Flag"]])` keeps the row labels (the feature names) where the flag is `True`.
  * *Hints:*
    * Write and test each stage as a few lines first, then move them into the function.
    * Use `int(...)` around counts so the dictionary prints plain numbers.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Correct path: `{'records': 10, 'coupons': 5, 'method': 'model', 'drift_features': ['Monthly_Minutes_Count'], 'needs_review': True}`.
    * Wrong path: `{'records': 10, 'coupons': 5, 'method': 'fallback rule', 'drift_features': ['Monthly_Minutes_Count'], 'needs_review': True}`.
    * Business meaning: even when the model runs, tonight still needs a person's review, because tonight's users listen far less than the users the model learned from. A decision system should report its own doubts, not only its decisions.

<a id="session-04-02"></a>
### Session 4.2: Turning Predictions into Money: Profit at Risk, Net Benefit, ROI, and the Best Offer

![Session 4.2 Map](assets/session_04_02_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_04_02_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_04_02_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 14 (115/12/13 - 115/12/19) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)
* **How this session is organized:** first the problem, then the result, then how a churn model and a little arithmetic produced that result, step by step with code. The three exercises come last.

* **1. The Problem:**
  * A drink shop near campus runs a membership app. Some members visit every week; others slowly stop coming. When a regular stops coming, the shop loses the **profit** that member brought in every month.
  * The shop can send an offer through the app to members who look likely to stop:
    * a **free-drink coupon**: costs the shop NT$45, and brings back 1 in 4 members who would otherwise stop;
    * a **buy-one-get-one month** ("BOGO"): costs NT$150, and brings back 45% of them.
  * Members are not equally valuable: one brings in NT$30 of profit a month, another NT$265. A member who is likely to stop but brings in little may not be worth an offer; a valuable member may be worth one even at a lower risk.
  * The shop owner asks: which members are about to stop coming, how much profit is at stake, and which offer should each member get so that the campaign pays for itself?

* **2. The Result:**
  * A churn model (churn: customers leaving) learned from 30 past members gives each of the 12 current members a chance of stopping. Multiplied by each member's yearly profit, this gives the **profit at risk**: NT$`7,355` for the 12 members.
  * Three ways to plan the campaign, compared on the same 12 members:

    | Plan | Members contacted | Net benefit (saved profit - cost) |
    |---|---|---|
    | Coupon for every member with a chance of `0.5` or more | 3 | NT$`689` (ROI `510.6`%) |
    | Coupon for every member with a chance of `0.3` or more | 7 | NT$`1,349` (ROI `428.2`%) |
    | Coupon only where its expected value is positive | 8 | NT$`1,441` |
    | The best offer for each member: BOGO, coupon, or none | 8 | NT$`2,059` |

    ROI (Return on Investment) = net benefit / cost x 100.
  * **Answer 1:** the members most likely to stop are not the ones with the most profit at stake. `NOW-203` has a high chance (`0.736`) but little profit; `NOW-204` has a lower chance (`0.396`) but the highest profit.
  * **Answer 2:** judge each member by expected money, not by a single cut-off. The highest ROI (`510.6`%) comes from the smallest campaign, but the largest profit comes from contacting more members.
  * **Answer 3:** give each member the offer with the highest expected net value: BOGO for 7 valuable members, a coupon for 1, and nothing for the 4 who are not at risk. This earns NT$`2,059`, about NT$`710` more than the best single cut-off.
  * **How it was done:** Sections 6 to 8 build it step by step; the exercises compare the plans.

* **3. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Who is likely to stop coming?": train a churn model on past members and score the current members
  2. "How much money is at stake?": calculate profit at risk (chance x yearly profit) and rank members by it
  3. "Which offer pays?": calculate the expected net value of an offer for each member, and a campaign's net benefit and ROI

* **4. Key Ideas: Expected Value, ROI:**
  * **Learn from the past, act on the present.** The history file holds past members whose outcome is known: they stopped coming or they stayed. The active file holds current members whose outcome is not known yet, the only ones an offer can still help. The model learns from the history and predicts for the active members (Sessions 3.4 and 4.1).
  * **Expected value: a chance times an amount.** If a member brings in NT$2,040 of profit a year and has a `0.748` chance of stopping, the profit we expect to lose is `0.748 x 2,040 = NT$1,526`: the **profit at risk**. This is not what will happen to this one member (they either stay or go), but it is the fair average over many members like them.
  * **The expected value of an offer, for one member:**

    | Part | Formula | `NOW-209`, free-drink coupon |
    |---|---|---|
    | Expected saved profit | chance x success rate x yearly profit | `0.748 x 0.25 x 2,040 = 381.48` |
    | Cost | the offer's cost | `45` |
    | Expected net value | saved profit - cost | `+336.48`: worth sending |

    An offer is worth sending when its expected net value is above `0`.
  * **Two summary numbers for a campaign:**
    * **Net benefit** = total saved profit - total cost, in NT$: how much the campaign adds to profit.
    * **ROI** = net benefit / cost x 100, in percent: how much each NT$1 spent brings back.
    * The plan with the highest ROI is often small; the plan with the highest net benefit is often larger. A shop that can afford the campaign should prefer the higher net benefit.

    ![The expected value of a coupon for one member: chance of stopping times success rate times yearly profit, minus the cost of the coupon](assets/session_04_02_fig_expected_value.png)

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * Two files (fictional teaching data):
    * History: [data/session_04_02_dataset.csv](data/session_04_02_dataset.csv), 30 past members with their outcome; 11 of them stopped coming.
    * Active: [data/session_04_02_active.csv](data/session_04_02_active.csv), 12 current members with the same columns except the outcome.
  * Columns:
    * `Member_ID`: Member code (`MEM-...` in the history, `NOW-...` for current members).
    * `Months_As_Member_Count`: Months since the member joined (`1` to `35`).
    * `Visits_Last_Month_Count`: Visits to the shop in the last month (`0` to `12`).
    * `Days_Since_Last_Visit_Count`: Days since the last visit (`5` to `53`).
    * `Monthly_Profit_TWD`: The shop's average profit from this member per month, after the cost of the drinks (`30` to `265`).
    * `Stopped_Visiting_Binary` (history only): `1` = stopped coming within 60 days, `0` = kept coming.
  * Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE: activate your `.venv` (Python Setup Clinic), name each script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S42_step_1.py`), run it with `python` followed by the file name, and check for return code `0`. On an error, paste the traceback into the AI Agent Chat and ask why it failed.

* **6. Step 1: Who Will Stop?:**
  * *Goal:* train a churn model on the 30 past members and give each of the 12 current members a chance of stopping.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S42_step_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: 30 past members with their outcome, and 12 current members
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    history = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_dataset.csv", index_col="Member_ID")
    active = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_active.csv", index_col="Member_ID")
    features = ["Months_As_Member_Count", "Visits_Last_Month_Count",
                "Days_Since_Last_Visit_Count"]

    # 2. Process: learn from the past members, then score the current ones
    model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
    model.fit(history[features], history["Stopped_Visiting_Binary"])
    active["Chance"] = model.predict_proba(active[features])[:, 1].round(3)

    # 3. Output: the current members, most likely to stop first
    ranked = active.sort_values("Chance", ascending=False)
    print(ranked[["Days_Since_Last_Visit_Count", "Chance"]])
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * Two files: `history` has the answers and trains the model; `active` has none and is scored. The model never sees the current members' outcomes, which do not exist yet.
    * The pipeline is the same as in Sessions 3.4 and 4.1: scale, then classify.
    * `.round(3)` keeps three decimals; the next steps use these rounded chances, so all numbers in this session match your screen.
    * `ranked[[...]]` shows one feature next to the chance, so you can see why a member scores high.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
               Days_Since_Last_Visit_Count  Chance
    Member_ID                                     
    NOW-209                             29   0.748
    NOW-203                             51   0.736
    NOW-201                             53   0.715
    NOW-210                             33   0.426
    NOW-204                             37   0.396
    NOW-212                             22   0.389
    NOW-205                             34   0.341
    NOW-202                             25   0.296
    NOW-207                              8   0.025
    NOW-208                              5   0.021
    NOW-206                             21   0.019
    NOW-211                              9   0.011
    ```

    * Three members are very likely to stop (chances above `0.7`); four are clearly safe (below `0.03`).
    * `NOW-201` and `NOW-203` have not been in for over 50 days. `NOW-209` comes first although its last visit was only 29 days ago: it made no visit at all last month.
  * *Common mistake:* training on the active members. They have no outcome yet, so there is nothing to learn from them; they are only scored.
  * *Quick Check 1 (the base rate):*
    * New tool: the mean of a 0/1 column is the share of 1s (Session 3.1). Why: the share of past members who stopped is the base rate that every chance is compared with.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Past stop rate:", round(history["Stopped_Visiting_Binary"].____(), 2))`
    * Check: `Past stop rate: 0.37`: 11 of the 30 past members stopped coming.

* **7. Step 2: Profit at Risk:**
  * *Goal:* turn each member's chance into money: one year of profit, and the part of it that is at risk.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S42_step_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: score the current members, as in Step 1
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    history = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_dataset.csv", index_col="Member_ID")
    active = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_active.csv", index_col="Member_ID")
    features = ["Months_As_Member_Count", "Visits_Last_Month_Count",
                "Days_Since_Last_Visit_Count"]
    model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
    model.fit(history[features], history["Stopped_Visiting_Binary"])
    active["Chance"] = model.predict_proba(active[features])[:, 1].round(3)

    # 2. Process: one year of profit, and the part of it that is at risk
    active["Annual_Profit"] = active["Monthly_Profit_TWD"] * 12
    active["Profit_At_Risk"] = (active["Chance"] * active["Annual_Profit"]).round(0)

    # 3. Output: the members ranked by profit at risk, and the total
    ranked = active.sort_values("Profit_At_Risk", ascending=False)
    print(ranked[["Chance", "Annual_Profit", "Profit_At_Risk"]])
    total = active["Profit_At_Risk"].sum()
    print(f"Total profit at risk: NT${total:,.0f}")
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * `Monthly_Profit_TWD * 12` gives one year of profit per member. A member's whole value over many years is called **CLV** (Customer Lifetime Value); one year is simpler and a cautious basis for spending decisions.
    * `Chance * Annual_Profit` is the expected loss: the profit at risk (section 4).
    * The ranking is now by money, not by chance.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
               Chance  Annual_Profit  Profit_At_Risk
    Member_ID                                       
    NOW-209     0.748           2040          1526.0
    NOW-201     0.715           1860          1330.0
    NOW-204     0.396           3180          1259.0
    NOW-212     0.389           2280           887.0
    NOW-205     0.341           1980           675.0
    NOW-202     0.296           1860           551.0
    NOW-210     0.426           1260           537.0
    NOW-203     0.736            600           442.0
    NOW-208     0.021           3180            67.0
    NOW-207     0.025           2580            64.0
    NOW-211     0.011            900            10.0
    NOW-206     0.019            360             7.0
    Total profit at risk: NT$7,355
    ```

    * `NOW-204` jumps to third: its chance is only `0.396`, but it brings in the most profit (NT$3,180 a year).
    * `NOW-203` falls to eighth: it is very likely to stop (`0.736`) but brings in only NT$600 a year.
    * `NOW-208` brings in as much as `NOW-204`, but it is safe (`0.021`), so little of its profit is at risk.
  * *Picture it:* the same 12 members, ranked by chance and ranked by profit at risk.

    ![The 12 current members ranked by chance of stopping and by profit at risk: NOW-203 falls from second to eighth, NOW-204 rises from fifth to third](assets/session_04_02_fig_chance_vs_risk.png)

  * *Common mistake:* counting each likely-to-stop member's full yearly profit as "at risk". A member with a `0.4` chance will, on average, cost 40% of their profit, not 100%.
  * *Quick Check 2 (where the risk sits):*
    * Tool: `.head(3)` on the ranked table.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(round(ranked["Profit_At_Risk"].____(3).sum() / total, 2))`
    * Check: `0.56`: the top 3 members hold 56% of the profit at risk.

* **8. Step 3: Is a Coupon Worth It:**
  * *Goal:* for each member, compare the expected saved profit of a free-drink coupon with its cost, and send it only where it pays.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S42_step_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: score the current members and add their profit, as in Step 2
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
    history = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_dataset.csv", index_col="Member_ID")
    active = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_02_active.csv", index_col="Member_ID")
    features = ["Months_As_Member_Count", "Visits_Last_Month_Count",
                "Days_Since_Last_Visit_Count"]
    model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
    model.fit(history[features], history["Stopped_Visiting_Binary"])
    active["Chance"] = model.predict_proba(active[features])[:, 1].round(3)
    active["Annual_Profit"] = active["Monthly_Profit_TWD"] * 12

    # 2. Process: the expected net value of a free-drink coupon for each member
    COUPON_COST = 45        # a free drink costs the shop NT$45
    SUCCESS_RATE = 0.25     # a coupon brings back 1 in 4 members who would stop
    saved = active["Chance"] * SUCCESS_RATE * active["Annual_Profit"]
    active["Coupon_Net"] = (saved - COUPON_COST).round(0)
    worth_it = active[active["Coupon_Net"] > 0]

    # 3. Output: each member's coupon value, then the campaign
    ranked = active.sort_values("Coupon_Net", ascending=False)
    print(ranked[["Chance", "Annual_Profit", "Coupon_Net"]])
    net = worth_it["Coupon_Net"].sum()
    print(f"Coupons worth sending: {len(worth_it)} of {len(active)}")
    print(f"Net benefit: NT${net:,.0f}")
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * Names in capital letters, such as `COUPON_COST`, are fixed settings (Session 3.4, Exercise 1).
    * `saved` multiplies three things per member: the chance they would stop, the chance the coupon brings them back, and their yearly profit.
    * `Coupon_Net` subtracts the coupon's cost; `worth_it` keeps the members where the result is positive.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
               Chance  Annual_Profit  Coupon_Net
    Member_ID                                   
    NOW-209     0.748           2040       336.0
    NOW-201     0.715           1860       287.0
    NOW-204     0.396           3180       270.0
    NOW-212     0.389           2280       177.0
    NOW-205     0.341           1980       124.0
    NOW-202     0.296           1860        93.0
    NOW-210     0.426           1260        89.0
    NOW-203     0.736            600        65.0
    NOW-208     0.021           3180       -28.0
    NOW-207     0.025           2580       -29.0
    NOW-206     0.019            360       -43.0
    NOW-211     0.011            900       -43.0
    Coupons worth sending: 8 of 12
    Net benefit: NT$1,441
    ```

    * A coupon pays for 8 members, including `NOW-202` with a chance of only `0.296`: it brings in enough profit to be worth NT$45.
    * For the 4 safe members, the coupon costs more than it is expected to save, even for the valuable `NOW-208`.
    * Net benefit NT$1,441: more than any single cut-off on the chance (Exercise 2).
  * *Common mistake:* sending an offer to every member "just in case". For the 4 safe members, each coupon loses about NT$30 to NT$45.
  * *Quick Check 3 (a weaker coupon):*
    * Tool: the `SUCCESS_RATE` setting.
    * Task: change `SUCCESS_RATE = 0.25` to `0.15`, a coupon that brings back fewer members.
    * Check: still 8 coupons worth sending, but the net benefit falls to NT$721. How well an offer works matters as much as who gets it.

* **9. Summary: Chance to Decision:**
  * Each step turned the model's output into something closer to a business decision:

    ![From a chance to a decision: chance of stopping, times yearly profit gives profit at risk, times success rate minus cost gives the net value of an offer, which decides who gets which offer](assets/session_04_02_fig_chance_to_decision.png)

    | Step | What we did | Result |
    |---|---|---|
    | 1. Score | churn model on 30 past members | a chance for each of 12 current members |
    | 2. Put a price on it | chance x yearly profit | NT$7,355 of profit at risk |
    | 3. Decide | send a coupon only where its expected net value is positive | 8 coupons, net NT$1,441 |
    | Exercises | the business case, ROI vs net benefit, and the best offer per member | up to NT$2,059 |

  * *Remember:* chance x money = expected value; send an offer only when its expected saved profit beats its cost; the highest ROI is not always the most profit.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [Scikit-Learn: Logistic Regression (predict_proba)](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/linear_model.html#logistic-regression).
  * [pandas: sort_values](https://pandas.pydata.org/docs/reference/api/pandas.DataFrame.sort_values.html).
  * [Harvard Business Review: The Value of Keeping the Right Customers](https://hbr.org/2014/10/the-value-of-keeping-the-right-customers).
  * [Investopedia: Return on Investment (ROI)](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/r/returnoninvestment.asp).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - One Cut-Off:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S42_case.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S42_case.py`).
  * *Step 2: Price a Coupon Campaign for Members at 0.5 or Above:*
    Copy part **1. Input** of Step 3, then add:
    ```python
    # 2. Process: target by one cut-off, then saved profit, cost, net benefit, ROI
    COUPON_COST = 45
    SUCCESS_RATE = 0.25
    targeted = active[active["Chance"] >= 0.5]
    saved = (targeted["Chance"] * SUCCESS_RATE * targeted["Annual_Profit"]).sum()
    cost = len(targeted) * COUPON_COST
    net_benefit = saved - cost
    roi_pct = net_benefit / cost * 100

    # 3. Output: the business case
    print(f"Members targeted: {len(targeted)}")
    print(f"Expected saved profit: NT${saved:,.2f}")
    print(f"Campaign cost: NT${cost:,.2f}")
    print(f"Net benefit: NT${net_benefit:,.2f} | ROI: {roi_pct:.1f}%")
    ```
    * `.sum()` adds up the expected saved profit of all targeted members.
    * `cost` is the number of coupons times their cost.
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, explain why we multiply by the chance of stopping instead of counting each targeted member's full yearly profit.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S42_case.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* `Members targeted: 3`; expected saved profit `NT$824.36`; cost `NT$135.00`; net benefit `NT$689.36`; ROI `510.6`%. A high ROI, but Step 3 earned twice as much by contacting 8 members chosen by value.

Then fix the line.

    Highest ROI: `0.7` (`510.6`%, tied with `0.5` and `0.6`; no member's chance lies between `0.5` and `0.7`). Highest net benefit: `0.3` (NT$`1,348.89`). The broader campaign earns NT$`659.53` more at a lower ROI: if the shop can afford it, net benefit is what adds to profit.

<a id="session-04-03"></a>
### Session 4.3: Setting Prices with Data: Price Elasticity, Profit Simulation, and Clearing Stock Before a Deadline

![Session 4.3 Map](assets/session_04_03_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_04_03_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_04_03_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 15 (115/12/20 - 115/12/26) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)
* **How this session is organized:** first the problem, then the result, then how a price test and a few simulations produced that result, step by step with code. The three exercises come last.

* **1. The Problem:**
  * The campus store sells 10 university products: a hoodie, T-shirts, caps, mugs, and more. The semester ends in **14 days**. After that, students go home, and anything left in stock is sold to a discount wholesaler for 40% of what it cost the store: a loss of 60% of the cost on every unsold item.
  * The prices were set by gut feeling. Some may be too low: the product would sell out anyway, so the store leaves profit on the table. Others may be too high: the product will not sell out before the semester ends.
  * A lower price sells more items but earns less on each one. Which effect is stronger depends on how strongly students react to price.
  * The store manager asks: how strongly do students react to price, which prices should go up and which down, and how do we clear the stock before the semester ends?

* **2. The Result:**
  * A 5-week price test on the hoodie measured how strongly students react: a **1% price rise loses about 1.55% of sales** (the price elasticity, `-1.55`).
  * Simulating price changes for the whole store, with that reaction:

    | Change to every price | Daily revenue | Daily profit |
    |---|---|---|
    | `-15`% | NT$`33,356` (the most) | NT$`17,063` |
    | no change | NT$`31,840` | NT$`18,620` |
    | `+5`% | NT$`30,841` | NT$`18,646` (the most) |

  * At today's sales, 5 of the 10 products will still have stock when the semester ends: the hoodie, T-shirt, tote bag, mug, and lanyard.
  * One price for each product, chosen for the whole 14 days and counting the loss on leftovers:

    | Plan | Profit over the 14 days |
    |---|---|
    | today's prices | NT$`242,372` |
    | the best price change for each product (5 cuts, 5 rises) | NT$`253,557`: NT$`11,185` more |

  * **Answer 1:** students react strongly to price (`-1.55`): a cut brings many more sales, so revenue rises, but the margin on each item falls.
  * **Answer 2:** profit, not revenue, is the goal. A store-wide cut brings in the most money but earns less; a small rise earns slightly more.
  * **Answer 3:** one rule for all products leaves money behind. Products that will not sell out in time need a cut to avoid leftover losses; products that will sell out anyway can carry a higher price (Exercise 3).
  * **How it was done:** Sections 6 to 8 measure the reaction, simulate profit, and find the slow products, step by step.

* **3. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "How strongly do customers react to price?": estimate the price elasticity of demand from a price test
  2. "Would a price change raise profit?": simulate units, revenue, and profit for several price changes before making them
  3. "How do we clear the stock in time?": find the products that will not sell out before a deadline, and calculate the price cut each one needs

* **4. Key Ideas: Margin, Elasticity, Stock:**
  * **Price, volume, and profit.** For the hoodie (`MER-101`):

    | Term | Meaning | Hoodie |
    |---|---|---|
    | Unit margin | price - unit cost: what the store keeps on one item | `890 - 420 = NT$470` |
    | Margin % | unit margin / price x 100 | `52.8`% |
    | Daily profit | unit margin x daily sales | `470 x 8 = NT$3,760` |
    | Days of stock | stock / daily sales: how long the stock lasts | `120 / 8 = 15.0` days |

  * **Price elasticity of demand.** The % change in sales for a 1% change in price. An elasticity of `-1.55` means a 1% rise loses about 1.55% of sales, and a 1% cut gains about 1.55%. Between `0` and `-1`, customers barely react; below `-1`, as here, they react strongly.
  * **Using the elasticity** for a small price change (up to about 20%): new daily sales = old daily sales x (1 + elasticity x price change). For a `+5`% change: `1 + (-1.55 x 0.05) = 0.9225`, so about 7.75% fewer sales.

    ![How a price change works: a higher price earns more on each item but sells fewer; profit is margin times sales](assets/session_04_03_fig_price_tradeoff.png)

  * **Profit is the goal, not revenue.** A cut can raise revenue and still lower profit, because each extra sale brings in a smaller margin.
  * **Stock and time.** If the days of stock are more than the days left, some items will be left over. They lose 60% of their cost, so a cut that sells them can earn more than keeping the price.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * Two files (fictional teaching data):
    * Products: [data/session_04_03_dataset.csv](data/session_04_03_dataset.csv), the 10 products of the campus store, one row per product.
    * Price test: [data/session_04_03_price_test.csv](data/session_04_03_price_test.csv), 5 weeks of tested hoodie prices.
  * Product columns:
    * `Product_ID`: Product code (`MER-101` to `MER-110`).
    * `Product_Name`: Hoodie, T-shirt, Cap, Tote bag, Mug, Water bottle, Notebook, Umbrella, Lanyard, Keychain.
    * `Price_TWD`: Today's price in NT$.
    * `Unit_Cost_TWD`: What one item costs the store.
    * `Stock_Units`: Items in stock.
    * `Daily_Units`: Average items sold per day at today's price.
  * Price test columns: `Test_Week` (1 to 5), `Product_ID`, `Test_Price_TWD`, and `Avg_Daily_Units` (average hoodies sold per day that week).
  * Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE: activate your `.venv` (Python Setup Clinic), name each script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S43_step_1.py`), run it with `python` followed by the file name, and check for return code `0`. On an error, paste the traceback into the AI Agent Chat and ask why it failed.

* **6. Step 1: Measure the Reaction:**
  * *Goal:* measure the price elasticity from the 5-week hoodie price test.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S43_step_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: 5 weeks of tested prices for the hoodie
    import numpy as np
    import pandas as pd
    test = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_03_price_test.csv")

    # 2. Process: a straight line through log(units) against log(price)
    slope, intercept = np.polyfit(np.log(test["Test_Price_TWD"]),
                                  np.log(test["Avg_Daily_Units"]), 1)
    elasticity = round(slope, 2)

    # 3. Output: the elasticity and what it means
    print("Price elasticity of demand:", elasticity)
    print(f"A 1% price rise loses about {abs(elasticity)}% of units sold.")
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * The store charged a different hoodie price each week (NT$790 to NT$990) and counted the hoodies sold per day.
    * `np.log(...)` takes the logarithm of every price and every sales count. A logarithm turns percentage changes into plain differences, so a straight line through the logs has a slope that reads as "% change in sales for a 1% change in price": the elasticity.
    * `np.polyfit(x, y, 1)` fits the best straight line (degree `1`) through the 5 points and returns its slope and intercept (Session 3.3 found such a line with Scikit-Learn).
    * `round(slope, 2)` keeps 2 decimals; `abs()` drops the minus sign for the sentence.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
    Price elasticity of demand: -1.55
    A 1% price rise loses about 1.55% of units sold.
    ```

    * `-1.55` is below `-1`: students react strongly to the hoodie's price.
    * A 10% rise would lose about 15.5% of sales. Whether that raises or lowers profit depends on the margin: Step 2 checks.
  * *Picture it:* the 5 test weeks, with the line that measures the reaction.

    ![Hoodie price test: five weeks of prices from NT$790 to NT$990 and the hoodies sold per day, with the fitted line; higher prices sell fewer hoodies](assets/session_04_03_fig_price_test.png)

  * *Common mistake:* measuring the reaction from a single price change. One week can be unusual (an exam week, a holiday); a line through several weeks is more reliable.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`.pct_change()`):*
    * New tool: `column.pct_change()` gives the change from the previous row, as a share (`0.063` = 6.3%). Why: shows the week-to-week reactions behind the single slope.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print((test[["Test_Price_TWD", "Avg_Daily_Units"]].__________() * 100).round(1))`
    * Check: the second row shows `6.3` (price up 6.3%) and `-11.3` (sales down 11.3%). The first row is `NaN`, because week 1 has no previous week. The weekly reactions vary; the line averages them.

* **7. Step 2: Simulate Profit:**
  * *Goal:* use the elasticity to predict the whole store's daily profit if every price changed by -10% to +10%.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S43_step_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the store's products and the elasticity from Step 1
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_03_dataset.csv", index_col="Product_ID")
    elasticity = -1.55

    # 2. Process: one function, used for every price change
    def daily_profit(price_change):
        price = df["Price_TWD"] * (1 + price_change)
        units = df["Daily_Units"] * (1 + elasticity * price_change)
        return round(((price - df["Unit_Cost_TWD"]) * units).sum())

    curve = {}
    for change in [-0.10, -0.05, 0.0, 0.05, 0.10]:
        curve[f"{change * 100:+.0f}%"] = daily_profit(change)

    # 3. Output: the whole store's daily profit at each price change
    for label, profit in curve.items():
        print(f"{label}: NT${profit:,}")
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * `elasticity = -1.55` uses the hoodie's value for every product. A real store would test each product group; the hoodie is our best estimate.
    * `daily_profit(price_change)` computes the new prices, the new daily sales (section 4), and the store's total daily profit: (price - cost) x sales, added up over the 10 products.
    * The loop runs the function for 5 price changes and stores each result in a dictionary, labeled like `+5%`. `f"{change * 100:+.0f}%"` writes the change with its sign.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
    -10%: NT$17,829
    -5%: NT$18,348
    +0%: NT$18,620
    +5%: NT$18,646
    +10%: NT$18,424
    ```

    * Profit rises from `-10`% up to `+5`%, then falls again at `+10`%: there is a best point in between.
    * A store-wide 10% cut loses about NT$790 of profit a day, even though it sells more.
    * A 5% rise earns only NT$26 more a day: the store-wide price is already close to its best. The bigger gains come from treating products differently (Exercise 3).
  * *Picture it:* daily revenue and daily profit for price changes from -15% to +15% (Exercise 1).

    ![Daily revenue falls steadily as prices rise, but daily profit peaks at +5%: the price that brings in the most money is not the one that earns the most](assets/session_04_03_fig_profit_curve.png)

  * *Common mistake:* choosing the price with the highest revenue. Revenue is highest with a 15% cut (Exercise 1), but profit is lower there: the store would work harder for less.
  * *Quick Check 2 (`max(..., key=...)`):*
    * New tool: `max(curve, key=curve.get)` returns the label with the highest value. Why: finds the best scenario without reading the list by eye.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print("Best change:", ___(curve, key=curve.get))`
    * Check: `Best change: +5%`.

* **8. Step 3: Check the Deadline:**
  * *Goal:* compare each product's days of stock with the 14 days left in the semester.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S43_step_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the store's products; the semester ends in 14 days
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_03_dataset.csv", index_col="Product_ID")
    days_left = 14

    # 2. Process: how many days the stock lasts at today's sales
    df["Days_Of_Stock"] = (df["Stock_Units"] / df["Daily_Units"]).round(1)
    too_slow = df[df["Days_Of_Stock"] > days_left]

    # 3. Output: the products that will still have stock when the semester ends
    columns = ["Product_Name", "Stock_Units", "Daily_Units", "Days_Of_Stock"]
    print(too_slow[columns])
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * `Stock_Units / Daily_Units` is how many days the stock lasts if sales stay as they are.
    * `df[df["Days_Of_Stock"] > days_left]` keeps the products that would still have items after 14 days (Session 1.4).
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
               Product_Name  Stock_Units  Daily_Units  Days_Of_Stock
    Product_ID                                                      
    MER-101          Hoodie          120          8.0           15.0
    MER-102         T-shirt          310         20.0           15.5
    MER-104        Tote bag          190         12.0           15.8
    MER-105             Mug           90          5.0           18.0
    MER-109         Lanyard          150          9.0           16.7
    ```

    * 5 of the 10 products will not sell out by the end of the semester.
    * The mug is the slowest: 18 days of stock for 14 days left, so 20 mugs would be left over.
    * The other 5 products will sell out in time; their prices could even go up.
  * *Picture it:* days of stock for every product, against the 14 days left.

    ![Days of stock for the 10 products against the 14 days left in the semester: the hoodie, T-shirt, tote bag, mug, and lanyard will not sell out](assets/session_04_03_fig_days_of_stock.png)

  * *Common mistake:* cutting the price of every product before a deadline. Half of these products will sell out anyway; a cut there only gives away margin.
  * *Quick Check 3 (`np.where()`):*
    * New tool: `np.where(condition, value_if_true, value_if_false)` fills a whole column with one of two values. Why: labels every product in one line.
    * Task: add `import numpy as np` to part **1. Input**, and one line at the end of part **2. Process**: `df["Action"] = np._____(df["Days_Of_Stock"] > days_left, "Markdown", "Keep")`. Then print `df["Action"].value_counts()`.
    * Check: `Markdown` `5`, `Keep` `5`.

* **9. Summary: Pricing with Data:**
  * Three short steps replace gut feeling with numbers:

    ![Pricing with data: a price test measures the reaction, a simulation finds the price with the most profit, days of stock show which products need a cut, and the season plan counts the loss on leftovers](assets/session_04_03_fig_pricing_steps.png)

    | Step | What we did | Result |
    |---|---|---|
    | 1. Measure the reaction | a line through the logs of the hoodie price test | elasticity `-1.55` |
    | 2. Simulate profit | daily profit for price changes from -10% to +10% | best store-wide change `+5`%; a cut earns less |
    | 3. Check the deadline | days of stock against 14 days left | 5 products need a cut |
    | Exercises | revenue vs profit, markdowns, and the best price per product | NT$11,185 more over 14 days |

  * *Remember:* measure how customers react before changing a price; simulate profit, not revenue; count the cost of what will be left over.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [NumPy: `numpy.polyfit`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.polyfit.html).
  * [NumPy: `numpy.where`](https://numpy.org/doc/stable/reference/generated/numpy.where.html).
  * [pandas: `pct_change`](https://pandas.pydata.org/docs/reference/api/pandas.DataFrame.pct_change.html).
  * [Investopedia: Price Elasticity of Demand](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/p/priceelasticity.asp).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - Revenue or Profit?:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S43_curve.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S43_curve.py`).
  * *Step 2: Simulate a Wider Range of Price Changes:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the store's products and the measured elasticity
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_03_dataset.csv", index_col="Product_ID")
    elasticity = -1.55

    # 2. Process: today's margins, then units, revenue, and profit from -15% to +15%
    margin = df["Price_TWD"] - df["Unit_Cost_TWD"]
    df["Margin_Pct"] = (margin / df["Price_TWD"] * 100).round(1)
    rows = []
    for change in [-0.15, -0.10, -0.05, 0.0, 0.05, 0.10, 0.15]:
        price = df["Price_TWD"] * (1 + change)
        units = df["Daily_Units"] * (1 + elasticity * change)
        rows.append({
            "Change_Pct": round(change * 100),
            "Daily_Units": round(units.sum(), 1),
            "Revenue_TWD": round((price * units).sum()),
            "Profit_TWD": round(((price - df["Unit_Cost_TWD"]) * units).sum()),
        })
    curve = pd.DataFrame(rows)

    # 3. Output: margins today, then the curve
    print("Margin % by product:", df["Margin_Pct"].tolist())
    print(curve)
    ```
    * `rows.append({...})` adds one dictionary per price change; `pd.DataFrame(rows)` turns the list into a table (Session 3.3, Exercise 3).
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, explain why the price change with the highest revenue is not the one with the highest profit.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S43_curve.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* margins range from `52.8`% (hoodie) to `68.8`% (lanyard). Profit peaks at `+5` (NT$`18,646`), while revenue is highest at `-15` (NT$`33,356`): the 15% cut sells the most items (`123.2` a day) but earns the least profit (NT$`17,063`).

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Markdowns:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S43_markdown.py`, start from Step 3 and calculate the price cut each slow product needs to sell out in 14 days.
  * *Task 1 (Required Price Change):* For the products in `too_slow`, add:
    * `Needed_Daily_Units` = `Stock_Units` / 14: the daily sales needed to sell out in time
    * `Change_Pct` = (`Needed_Daily_Units` / `Daily_Units` - 1) / elasticity x 100, rounded to 1 decimal. This reverses the rule of section 4: if sales must rise by a given share, the price must change by that share divided by the elasticity (`-1.55`). The result is negative: a cut.
  * *Task 2 (Read the SyntaxError & AI Diagnosis):* Write the new price line with a missing bracket: `too_slow["New_Price_TWD"] = too_slow["Price_TWD"] * (1 + too_slow["Change_Pct"] / 100`. Run the script: Python stops before running anything, with `SyntaxError: '(' was never closed`. Capture the error (Windows: `Win + Shift + S`; Mac: `Cmd + Ctrl + Shift + 4`), paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat), and ask how to find the missing bracket. Antigravity highlights the matching bracket when you place the cursor next to one.
  * *Task 3 (Fix and Save):* Close the bracket, round `New_Price_TWD` to whole NT$, and save `Product_Name`, `Price_TWD`, `Change_Pct`, and `New_Price_TWD` for the slow products to `data/markdown_plan.csv`. Use `too_slow = df[...].copy()` in Step 3's code so that adding columns does not raise a warning.
  * *Success Check:*

    | Product | Price | Change | New price |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | Hoodie | NT$890 | `-4.6`% | NT$`849` |
    | T-shirt | NT$390 | `-6.9`% | NT$`363` |
    | Tote bag | NT$290 | `-8.4`% | NT$`266` |
    | Mug | NT$350 | `-18.4`% | NT$`286` |
    | Lanyard | NT$80 | `-12.3`% | NT$`70` |

    The deepest cut goes to the product with the most stock relative to its sales (the mug), not to the most expensive one (the hoodie).

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - Best Price per Product:**
  * *Goal:* Find, for each product, the price that earns the most over the 14 days left, counting the loss on any stock left over. Some prices may go up and some down. Show the manager how much the plan adds compared with keeping today's prices.
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_04_03_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S43_plan.py`.
    * Tools you already know: functions, `for` loops, `min()`, lists of dictionaries, and `pd.DataFrame()`.
  * *Rules of the season:*
    * 14 days left; elasticity `-1.55`; daily sales at a new price: daily sales x (1 + elasticity x change).
    * Items sold in 14 days: the smaller of (daily sales x 14) and the stock: the store cannot sell items it does not have.
    * Unsold items go to the wholesaler at 40% of their cost: a loss of `0.6 x unit cost` each.
    * Season profit = (price - unit cost) x items sold - 0.6 x unit cost x unsold items.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. Write `season_profit(price, unit_cost, daily_units, stock)` that returns the season profit, following the rules above.
    2. For each product, try the price changes `-20%`, `-15%`, `-10%`, `-5%`, `0%`, `+5%`, `+10%`, `+15%`, and `+20%`. Round each new price to whole NT$ before you use it.
    3. Keep, for each product, the change with the highest season profit (if two tie, keep the first one tried), and also record the season profit at `0%`.
    4. Build one DataFrame with `Product_Name`, `Best_Change_Pct`, `Best_Price_TWD`, `Best_Profit_TWD`, and `Today_Profit_TWD`, profits rounded to whole NT$.
    5. Print the table, the total of both profit columns, and the difference.
    6. Count how many products get a cut, a rise, or no change.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Looping over rows: `for product_id, row in df.iterrows():`. Each `row` works like a small dictionary: `row["Unit_Cost_TWD"]`.
    * `min(a, b)` returns the smaller of two numbers.
  * *Hints:*
    * Test `season_profit()` on the hoodie at `0%` first: 112 hoodies sold in 14 days, 8 left over, so `470 x 112 - 0.6 x 420 x 8 = NT$50,624`.
    * Inside the product loop, keep the best result so far in two variables and update them when a change earns more.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Cuts: hoodie, T-shirt, and tote bag (`-5`% each), mug and lanyard (`-10`% each). Rises: notebook and umbrella (`+5`% each); cap, water bottle, and keychain (`+10`% each).
    * Total profit over the 14 days: NT$`253,557` with the plan, against NT$`242,372` at today's prices: NT$`11,185` more.
    * Business meaning: the five cuts go exactly to the products that would not sell out (Step 3), and none of them needs the full markdown of Exercise 2: the mug does best at `-10`%, not `-18.4`%, because a few leftover mugs cost less than a deep cut on every mug sold.
