<a id="session-04-04"></a>
### Session 4.4: Is the AI Fair? Auditing a Screening Tool: Group Rates, the Four-Fifths Rule, Explanations, and Governance

![Session 4.4 Map](assets/session_04_04_map.png)

* **Lecture Presentation:** [Download Slides (PDF)](slides/session_04_04_lecture.pdf)
* **Reading (PDF):** [Download Reading (PDF)](handouts/session_04_04_reading.pdf): read before class
* **Schedule:** Week 16 (115/12/27 - 116/01/02) | In-person classes (1:2 Delivery Ratio: 60 Min Lecture + 120 Min Lab)
* **How this session is organized:** first the problem, then the result, then how a fairness audit produced that result, step by step with code. The three exercises come last.

* **1. The Problem:**
  * A technology company receives many applications for its summer internship. An AI screening tool gives every applicant a **screening score** from 0 to 1, and applicants with `0.70` or more are invited to an interview. Nobody reads the other applications.
  * The tool looks at three things: GPA, the score on an online coding test, and the number of club activities (clubs, student associations, volunteer roles) listed in the application.
  * The applicants come from two groups, `Group_A` and `Group_B`. The group is a **protected characteristic**, such as sex, that the law and the company's own rules forbid using against an applicant. The tool never sees the group; the company keeps it only to check fairness.
  * You are likely to meet tools like this when you apply for internships and jobs yourself.
  * The head of recruiting asks: does the tool invite both groups at similar rates, why was each rejected applicant rejected, and what should the company do before using the tool again?

* **2. The Result:**
  * On 20 applicants, 10 in each group:

    | Group | Invited | Invite rate |
    |---|---|---|
    | Group A | 7 of 10 | `70`% |
    | Group B | 3 of 10 | `30`% |

    The ratio of the two rates is `0.30 / 0.70 = 0.429`, far below `0.80`: the tool **fails the four-fifths rule**, a common first test of fairness.
  * The two groups have almost the same skills: an average GPA of `3.31` and `3.35`, and an average coding score of `70.7` and `70.1`. The difference is in club activities: `3.4` against `1.1` on average.
  * Explaining each rejection shows the main reason: for 5 of the 7 rejected Group B applicants, it was **too few club activities**, not a low GPA or coding score. `APP-315` (Group B, coding `78`, GPA `3.5`, no clubs) was rejected, while `APP-319` (Group A, coding `64`, GPA `3.0`, 5 clubs) was invited.
  * **Answer 1:** no. Group B is invited far less often, although its skills are the same on average.
  * **Answer 2:** each rejection can be explained by the effect of each feature; the club count is behind most Group B rejections. It may measure free time (for example, students who need a part-time job have less time for clubs) more than ability.
  * **Answer 3:** changing the threshold does not fix it (Exercise 3). The company should review whether club activities belong in the score, add a person's review of near-miss applicants, and write down every decision.
  * **How it was done:** Sections 6 to 8 do the audit step by step.

* **3. Expected Learning Outcomes (By the end of this session, you will be able to):**
  1. "Are the groups treated alike?": calculate each group's selection rate and the ratio of the rates, and apply the four-fifths rule
  2. "Where does a gap come from?": compare the groups' features, and explain each decision with feature effects
  3. "What should the company do?": test whether a threshold change can fix a gap, and write a governance record

* **4. Key Ideas: Bias, a Test, Explanations:**
  * **How a fair-looking tool can be unfair.** A model learns patterns from past data. The tool never sees the group, but if the groups differ on a feature that the tool uses, the tool's decisions will differ too. Nobody intended it.

    ![The screening tool never sees the group, but uses club activities; Group B has fewer club activities on average, so its scores and invite rate are lower, although GPA and coding scores are the same](assets/session_04_04_fig_proxy.png)

  * **Two kinds of unfair treatment:**
    * **Disparate treatment:** deciding differently because of a person's group, for example a different threshold for each group. This is forbidden in hiring in many countries, so it can never be the fix.
    * **Disparate impact:** one rule for everyone on paper, but one group is selected far less often. It is a warning sign that must be investigated and justified.
  * **The four-fifths rule.** If one group's selection rate is less than 80% (four-fifths) of the highest group's rate, the gap needs review. It comes from US hiring guidelines (1978) and is widely used as a first test. Failing it is a reason to investigate, not proof of discrimination.
  * **Explaining a decision.** For a model that adds up its features, each feature's **effect** on one applicant = coefficient x (applicant's value - average value). A negative effect pulled the score down; the most negative one is the main reason.
  * **A copy of a hidden model.** The company bought the tool and does not know its formula. We fit a linear regression that copies the scores from the three features (a **surrogate model**) and explain the copy. Its explanations can be trusted only if the copy is accurate (Step 3).
  * **Governance.** The company stays responsible for its automated decisions. Laws such as the EU AI Act (European Union Artificial Intelligence Act) treat AI used in hiring as high-risk: it needs documentation, human oversight, and transparency. A written **governance record** states what was tested, what was found, and who decided what.

* **5. Lab Practice Dataset:**
  * File Link: [data/session_04_04_dataset.csv](data/session_04_04_dataset.csv)
  * 20 internship applicants (fictional teaching data), one row per applicant, 10 in each group:
    * `Applicant_ID`: Applicant code (`APP-301` to `APP-320`).
    * `Group`: The protected group, anonymized as `Group_A` or `Group_B`. The tool does not use it; auditors use it only to test fairness.
    * `GPA`: Grade point average, on a scale up to 4.3.
    * `Coding_Test_Score`: Score on the online coding test, from 0 to 100.
    * `Club_Activities_Count`: Club activities listed in the application (`0` to `5`).
    * `Screening_Score`: The AI tool's score, from 0 to 1; invited to an interview at `0.70` or more.
  * Workflow in Google Antigravity IDE: activate your `.venv` (Python Setup Clinic), name each script with your name and the session code (for example, `Reacher_S44_step_1.py`), run it with `python` followed by the file name, and check for return code `0`. On an error, paste the traceback into the AI Agent Chat and ask why it failed.

* **6. Step 1: Compare the Rates:**
  * *Goal:* turn the scores into decisions, compare the invite rates of the two groups, and apply the four-fifths rule.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S44_step_1.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: 20 internship applicants and their AI screening scores
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_04_dataset.csv", index_col="Applicant_ID")

    # 2. Process: invited at 0.70, the invite rate of each group, and the ratio
    df["Invited"] = (df["Screening_Score"] >= 0.70).astype(int)
    rates = df.groupby("Group")["Invited"].mean()
    ratio = rates.min() / rates.max()

    # 3. Output: rates, the ratio, and the four-fifths test
    print(rates)
    print(f"Ratio of the invite rates: {ratio:.3f}")
    print("Passes the four-fifths rule:", ratio >= 0.80)
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * `(df["Screening_Score"] >= 0.70).astype(int)` turns each score into `1` (invited) or `0` (rejected) (Session 3.4).
    * `df.groupby("Group")["Invited"].mean()` gives each group's invite rate: the mean of a 0/1 column is the share of 1s (Session 3.1).
    * `rates.min() / rates.max()` divides the lower rate by the higher one, whichever group it is.
    * `ratio >= 0.80` gives `True` or `False`: the four-fifths test.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
    Group
    Group_A    0.7
    Group_B    0.3
    Name: Invited, dtype: float64
    Ratio of the invite rates: 0.429
    Passes the four-fifths rule: False
    ```

    * 7 of 10 Group A applicants are invited, but only 3 of 10 in Group B.
    * The ratio, `0.429`, is far below `0.80`: the gap must be investigated before the tool is used again.
  * *Picture it:* the invite rates of the two groups, and the four-fifths line.

    ![Invite rates: Group A 70 percent, Group B 30 percent; four-fifths of Group A's rate is 56 percent, and Group B is far below it](assets/session_04_04_fig_rates.png)

  * *Common mistake:* concluding "the tool never sees the group, so it is fair". Step 2 shows how a gap appears anyway.
  * *Quick Check 1 (`pd.crosstab()`):*
    * Tool: `pd.crosstab(row_column, column_column)` counts each combination of two columns (Session 3.2, Exercise 2). Why: the board wants counts, not only rates.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(pd.________(df["Group"], df["Invited"]))`
    * Check: Group A: `3` rejected (`0`), `7` invited (`1`); Group B: `7` rejected, `3` invited.

* **7. Step 2: Find the Cause:**
  * *Goal:* compare the two groups on the three features that the tool uses, and on the score.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S44_step_2.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the 20 applicants
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_04_dataset.csv", index_col="Applicant_ID")
    features = ["GPA", "Coding_Test_Score", "Club_Activities_Count"]

    # 2. Process: the average of each feature and of the score, by group
    profile = df.groupby("Group")[features + ["Screening_Score"]].mean().round(2)

    # 3. Output: the two groups side by side
    print(profile.T)
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * `features + ["Screening_Score"]` joins two lists: the three features and the score.
    * `df.groupby("Group")[...].mean()` averages each column within each group (Session 1.4).
    * `profile.T` flips the table (`T` for "transpose"): the measures become rows and the two groups become columns, which is easier to compare.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
    Group                  Group_A  Group_B
    GPA                       3.31     3.35
    Coding_Test_Score        70.70    70.10
    Club_Activities_Count     3.40     1.10
    Screening_Score           0.75     0.62
    ```

    * GPA and coding scores are almost the same in both groups: on the skills the job needs, the groups are alike.
    * Club activities differ a lot: `3.4` against `1.1`. The tool gives points for clubs, so Group B scores lower on average (`0.62` against `0.75`).
    * The real question for people, not for data: does the number of clubs measure ability for this job, or does it measure free time? Students who work part-time jobs or care for family members have less time for clubs.
  * *Picture it:* the three features, group by group.

    ![Average GPA, coding score, and club activities for Group A and Group B: GPA and coding scores are almost equal; club activities differ by more than three times](assets/session_04_04_fig_profiles.png)

  * *Common mistake:* removing the group column and believing the problem is solved. The tool never had the group column; the gap comes through a feature that differs between the groups (a **proxy**).
  * *Quick Check 2 (the spread inside each group):*
    * Tool: `.min()` and `.max()` with `groupby()`.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(df.groupby("Group")["Club_Activities_Count"].agg(["min", "max"]))`
    * Check: Group A `1` to `5`; Group B `0` to `3`. Three Group B applicants listed no club at all; every Group A applicant listed at least one.

* **8. Step 3: Explain Each Decision:**
  * *Goal:* copy the hidden score with a linear model, then show how much each feature pushed each rejected applicant's score down, and name the main reason.
  * *Code:* `YourName_S44_step_3.py`
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the 20 applicants and the three features
    import pandas as pd
    from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_04_dataset.csv", index_col="Applicant_ID")
    features = ["GPA", "Coding_Test_Score", "Club_Activities_Count"]

    # 2. Process: copy the hidden score, then the effect of each feature
    copy = LinearRegression().fit(df[features], df["Screening_Score"])
    effects = ((df[features] - df[features].mean()) * copy.coef_).round(3)
    effects.columns = ["GPA", "Test", "Clubs"]
    effects["Main_Reason"] = effects.idxmin(axis=1)
    rejected = df["Screening_Score"] < 0.70

    # 3. Output: how well the copy fits, then the reasons for each rejection
    fit = copy.score(df[features], df["Screening_Score"])
    print("R-squared of the copy:", round(fit, 3))
    print(effects[rejected].join(df["Group"]))
    ```
  * *How it works, line by line:*
    * `LinearRegression().fit(...)` learns a formula that copies the tool's scores from the three features: the surrogate (Session 3.3).
    * `(df[features] - df[features].mean()) * copy.coef_` computes every applicant's effect for all three features at once: each column's average is subtracted, and each column is multiplied by its own coefficient.
    * `effects.columns = [...]` gives the three effects short names.
    * `effects.idxmin(axis=1)` returns, for each row (`axis=1`), the name of the column with the most negative value: the main reason.
    * `effects[rejected].join(df["Group"])` keeps the rejected applicants and adds their group, for the audit only.
  * *Read the output:*

    ```text
    R-squared of the copy: 0.987
                    GPA   Test  Clubs Main_Reason    Group
    Applicant_ID                                          
    APP-303       0.043 -0.080 -0.014        Test  Group_B
    APP-305      -0.068 -0.080  0.101        Test  Group_A
    APP-306      -0.052 -0.028 -0.072       Clubs  Group_B
    APP-307      -0.068 -0.086 -0.072        Test  Group_B
    APP-312      -0.005  0.010 -0.129       Clubs  Group_B
    APP-313      -0.036 -0.048 -0.129       Clubs  Group_B
    APP-315       0.027  0.049 -0.129       Clubs  Group_B
    APP-316      -0.084 -0.061 -0.072         GPA  Group_A
    APP-318      -0.036 -0.099  0.101        Test  Group_A
    APP-320      -0.021 -0.009 -0.072       Clubs  Group_B
    ```

    * The copy explains `98.7`% of the score differences, so its explanations can be trusted.
    * The 3 rejected Group A applicants were rejected for their coding score or GPA.
    * For 5 of the 7 rejected Group B applicants, the main reason is club activities. `APP-315` had a better GPA and coding score than average (`+0.027` and `+0.049`), but having no clubs (`-0.129`) pulled the score below `0.70`.
  * *Picture it:* `APP-315` (rejected) and `APP-319` (invited), feature by feature.

    ![Feature effects for APP-315, rejected, and APP-319, invited: APP-315 has positive GPA and coding effects but a large negative club effect; APP-319 has negative GPA and coding effects but a large positive club effect](assets/session_04_04_fig_two_applicants.png)

  * *Common mistake:* explaining a model you have not checked. A surrogate's explanations are only as good as its fit.
  * *Quick Check 3 (counting the main reasons):*
    * Tool: `pd.crosstab()` again.
    * Task: add one line to part **3. Output**: `print(pd.crosstab(df.loc[rejected, "Group"], effects.loc[rejected, "Main_Reason"]))`
    * Check: Group A: GPA `1`, Test `2`, Clubs `0`; Group B: Clubs `5`, Test `2`.

* **9. Summary: The Audit:**
  * The same three steps work for any automated decision about people:

    ![A fairness audit: compare selection rates and apply the four-fifths rule, compare the groups' features to find the cause, explain each decision, then decide and document](assets/session_04_04_fig_audit_steps.png)

    | Step | What we did | Result |
    |---|---|---|
    | 1. Compare rates | invite rate by group; four-fifths rule | `70`% vs `30`%; ratio `0.429`: fails |
    | 2. Find the cause | average features by group | same skills; club activities `3.4` vs `1.1` |
    | 3. Explain decisions | feature effects from a surrogate (R-squared `0.987`) | clubs is the main reason for 5 of 7 Group B rejections |
    | Exercises | an audit report, a governance record, a threshold test | the threshold cannot fix the gap |

  * *What a fix may and may not do:*

    | Possible fix | Allowed? | Why |
    |---|---|---|
    | A lower threshold for Group B | no | deciding by group is disparate treatment |
    | One lower threshold for everyone | only with care | Exercise 3: it invites applicants below the coding bar |
    | Review whether club activities belong in the score | yes | addresses the cause |
    | A person reviews applicants just below `0.70` | yes | human oversight of near misses |
    | Document every test and decision | yes | the company stays accountable |

  * *Remember:* a tool can be unfair without seeing the group; compare rates, find the feature behind the gap, explain each decision, never decide by group, and write everything down.

* **10. Official Python Documentation & References:**
  * [European Commission: Regulatory Framework for Artificial Intelligence (EU AI Act)](https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/regulatory-framework-ai).
  * [US Equal Employment Opportunity Commission: Uniform Guidelines, Questions and Answers (the four-fifths rule)](https://www.eeoc.gov/laws/guidance/questions-and-answers-clarify-and-provide-common-interpretation-uniform-guidelines).
  * [Fairlearn: A Python Toolkit for Assessing and Improving Fairness in AI](https://fairlearn.org/).
  * [Barocas, Hardt, Narayanan: Fairness and Machine Learning](https://fairmlbook.org/).

* **11. Exercise 1: Guided Practice - The Audit Report:**
  * *Step 1: Open Script:* In Google Antigravity IDE, create a new script named `YourName_S44_audit.py`, replacing `YourName` with your own name (for example, `Reacher_S44_audit.py`).
  * *Step 2: Write the Report:*
    Type the following code:
    ```python
    # 1. Input: the 20 applicants
    import pandas as pd
    df = pd.read_csv("data/session_04_04_dataset.csv", index_col="Applicant_ID")

    # 2. Process: decisions in words, counts per group, rates, and the ratio
    invited = df["Screening_Score"] >= 0.70
    df["Decision"] = invited.map({True: "Invited", False: "Rejected"})
    counts = pd.crosstab(df["Group"], df["Decision"])
    rates = counts["Invited"] / counts.sum(axis=1)
    ratio = rates.min() / rates.max()

    # 3. Output: the audit findings
    print(counts)
    rate_a, rate_b = rates["Group_A"], rates["Group_B"]
    print(f"Group A: {rate_a:.0%} invited | Group B: {rate_b:.0%} invited")
    print(f"Ratio of the invite rates: {ratio:.3f}")
    if ratio < 0.80:
        print("ALERT: fails the four-fifths rule; investigate before using it again.")
    else:
        print("Passes the four-fifths rule.")
    ```
    * `.map({True: "Invited", False: "Rejected"})` replaces each value using a dictionary: reports for people should use words, not codes.
    * `counts.sum(axis=1)` adds up each row: the number of applicants per group.
    * `{rate_a:.0%}` prints a share as a whole percent.
  * *Step 3: Interpret:* In two comment lines, explain why failing the four-fifths rule calls for an investigation, and why it does not by itself prove that the tool discriminates.
  * *Step 4: Execute in Terminal:* Run `python YourName_S44_audit.py` in Panel `[4]` and confirm return code `0`.
  * *Success Check:* the table shows Group A `7` invited and `3` rejected, Group B `3` invited and `7` rejected; `Group A: 70% invited | Group B: 30% invited`; ratio `0.429`; the `ALERT` line prints.

* **12. Exercise 2: Applied Practice - Audit Function and Record:**
  * *Coding Challenge:* In `YourName_S44_record.py`, turn the audit into a function and save a governance record for the company's management.
  * *Task 1 (Audit Function):* Write `screening_audit(df, threshold)` that computes the invite rate of each group and `ratio` at any threshold, as in Step 1.
  * *Task 2 (Read the NameError & AI Diagnosis):* Call `screening_audit(df, 0.70)`, then write `print(ratio)` on the next line, outside the function. The script stops with `NameError: name 'ratio' is not defined`. Capture the traceback (Windows: `Win + Shift + S`; Mac: `Cmd + Ctrl + Shift + 4`), paste it into Panel `[3]` (AI Agent Chat), and ask why a variable created inside a function is not available outside it.
  * *Task 3 (Return, Record, Save):* Make the function end with `return rates, ratio`, and call it as `rates, ratio = screening_audit(df, 0.70)`. Build a one-row governance record with `pd.DataFrame([{...}])` and the keys `Tool`, `Threshold`, `Group_A_Rate`, `Group_B_Rate`, `Ratio` (rounded to 3 decimals), `Passes_Four_Fifths`, `Main_Finding`, and `Status`. Set `Status` to `"Review before use"` if the test fails, otherwise `"Approved for use"`, and `Main_Finding` to `"Club activities drive most Group B rejections"`. Save it to `data/governance_record.csv` with `index=False` and print it.
  * *Success Check:* the record shows `Threshold` `0.7`, `Group_A_Rate` `0.7`, `Group_B_Rate` `0.3`, `Ratio` `0.429`, `Passes_Four_Fifths` `False`, and `Status` `Review before use`; the script ends with return code `0`.

* **13. Exercise 3: Advanced Practice - One Shared Threshold?:**
  * *Goal:* A different threshold for each group would be disparate treatment, so it is not an option. Test whether a **single threshold for everyone** can pass the four-fifths rule, and what it would cost: the company does not want to invite applicants who scored below `60` on the coding test (its skill bar).
  * *What you need:*
    * File: `data/session_04_04_dataset.csv` (described in **5. Lab Practice Dataset**).
    * Script name: `YourName_S44_threshold.py`.
    * Tools you already know: your `screening_audit()` function from Exercise 2, `for` loops, lists of dictionaries, `pd.DataFrame()`, and True/False masks.
  * *Requirements (follow them in order):*
    1. For each shared threshold in `[0.50, 0.55, 0.60, 0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80]`, compute the number invited, the invite rate of each group, the ratio (rounded to 3 decimals), and `Below_Skill_Bar`: invited applicants with a coding score below `60`.
    2. Collect the results in one DataFrame with the columns `Threshold`, `Invited`, `Group_A_Rate`, `Group_B_Rate`, `Ratio`, and `Below_Skill_Bar`.
    3. Find the **highest** threshold that passes the four-fifths rule, and report its `Below_Skill_Bar`.
    4. Find the **highest** threshold that passes the four-fifths rule **and** has `0` applicants below the skill bar. If there is none, print that clearly.
    5. Write three comment lines for management: what a threshold can and cannot fix, and two other actions the company could take instead.
  * *New tools for this exercise:*
    * Combining two masks: `invited & (df["Coding_Test_Score"] < 60)` is `True` for applicants who are invited **and** below the skill bar; `.sum()` counts them.
    * Filtering a results table with two conditions: `results[(results["Ratio"] >= 0.80) & (results["Below_Skill_Bar"] == 0)]`. An empty result has a `len()` of `0`.
  * *Hints:*
    * Reuse `screening_audit()` inside the loop, so that every threshold is tested the same way.
    * Print the whole table before answering requirements 3 and 4.
  * *Self-Check (expected results):*
    * Ratios: `0.50` `0.889`, `0.55` `0.778`, `0.60` `0.556`, `0.65` `0.429`, `0.70` `0.429`, `0.75` `0.5`, `0.80` `0.25`.
    * Highest passing threshold: `0.50`, with 17 of 20 invited and `3` below the skill bar (`APP-303`, `APP-305`, `APP-318`).
    * No threshold passes the rule with `0` applicants below the skill bar.
    * Business meaning: moving one threshold either fails the fairness test or invites applicants who do not meet the skill bar. The threshold cannot fix the gap, because the gap comes from a feature. The company should review the club feature, have a person review near misses, and document the decision.
